Second Chair · saved records

Native topic record

Justice access: Direct AI use shifts risk downward

Corpus synthesis: Balances scalable help against weak remedies and users’ limited verification capacity, comparing imperfect assistance with no help.

This record retains the provider’s full available topic findings and source perspectives. It does not claim a new independent analytical review. Positions refer to this topic’s proposition: “Redirects” is not simple opposition, and “Unclear” is not support.

Declared lineage: Legal-service market restructuring: Bounded AI gains become structural change through institutional redesign · Legal-service restructuring: Bounded automation redistributes work, value and risk

149 distinct stance-bearing works · Builds on: 30 · Redirects: 13 · Supports: 83 · Unclear: 23

Native topic findings

  1. AI may narrow the justice gap by expanding provider capacity and offering scalable assistance to unrepresented people.
  2. The evidence supports task-specific augmentation, not uniform improvement or wholesale lawyer replacement.
  3. Greater access to legal information and filing does not necessarily produce effective remedies.
  4. Direct-to-consumer use concentrates reliability and verification risks among users least able to manage them.
  5. The central disagreement is whether imperfect AI should be judged against professional service or the realistic alternative of no assistance.
  6. AI could widen inequality even as it lowers the cost of some legal assistance.
  7. The dominant practical model combines narrow AI tools with human judgment, task-specific verification and accountable escalation.
  8. Regulation must balance consumer protection with room for supervised experimentation, while recognising that AI cannot substitute for broader justice-system reform.

Recorded source perspectives

Open a source to read its native account and every available returned quotation. Each source name also opens its saved work record.

RedirectsAugmented Lawyering

Augmented Lawyering

Academia & research · Automation debate (2015-2022)

Corpus framing of this source: Augmented Lawyering: deployment economics, human work and firm organisation

Corpus synthesis of this source: Augmented Lawyering argues that AI will substitute for some legal tasks while augmenting lawyers whose work remains human-led. It focuses on deployment costs, data availability and the organisation of legal work rather than technical capability alone.

Corpus source-specific finding

The authors shift attention from wholesale lawyer replacement to changes in work and firm organisation.

Exact returned source excerpt

Much of the debate to date has focused on the capabilities of tech- nical systems, asking “can machines replace lawyers?” Some argue that the en- tire way in which legal services are delivered will be radically transformed,4 while others maintain that only a small fraction of the tasks performed by lawyers are capable of being automated.5 Yet while AI will surely render some roles re- dundant, humans will still be crucial for legal services for the foreseeable future.

Exact returned source excerpt

that understanding how AI will augment lawyering requires a focus on two inter- related aspects of these human dimensions: how the nature of legal services work will change, and how the firms that co-ordinate this work will be organized.

Corpus source-specific finding

The authors claim an empirical foundation, but the supplied excerpts do not present study methods or results.

Exact returned source excerpt

We develop and substantiate our analysis with what is to our knowledge the most comprehensive empirical study yet conducted into the implementation of AI in legal services.

Exact returned source excerpt

INST., 2019 REPORT ON THE STATE OF THE LEGAL MARKET 2 (2019) (defining productivity as hours billed per month and showing constant, or slightly declining, average billable hours per lawyer across the US over the period 2007-2018); LAW SOC’Y OF ENG.

Corpus source-specific finding

Deployment economics help explain which legal tasks attract AI adoption.

Exact returned source excerpt

This Article begins with the impact of AI on legal services work.7 Under- standing this requires an appreciation not only of what is technically possible, but also the economics and logistics of deployment.

Exact returned source excerpt

Today’s AI is based on ma- chine learning (“ML”), the core of which is the prediction of outcomes by iden- tifying complex statistical relationships within a dataset.8 Through the applica- tion of natural language processing (“NLP”), these methods can be applied to the unstructured textual data that forms the bedrock of legal materials.

Corpus source-specific finding

Task substitution can coexist with productivity gains for lawyers doing work that remains human-led.

Exact returned source excerpt

These are tasks for which relevant data are readily available and in which results can be scaled, such as discovery review, contract analytics, and legal research.9 To the extent that AI is deployed for such legal tasks, the technology sub- stitutes for humans.10 The technical and economic constraints on AI’s deploy- ment, however, mean that more specialized and unique tasks will for the fore- seeable future exclusively be performed by human lawyers.11 So too will client- facing activities, which require an understanding of social context on which con- sistent data are hard to gather.

Exact returned source excerpt

It will augment their productivity, serving to increase the value of their human capital.12

Corpus synthesis of this source: ChatGPT, Esq.: Recasting Unauthorized Practice of Law in the Era of Generative AI argues that unauthorized-practice restrictions obstruct potentially affordable legal assistance as AI increasingly performs legal work. Exact returned source excerpt

Exact returned source excerpt

A joint letter from the Department of Justice and the Federal Trade Commission concluded, "There is no evidence before the [ABA] of which we are aware that consumers are hurt by this competition [between lawyers and nonlawyers] and there is substantial evidence that they benefit from it." Even 20 years ago, scholars recognized that UPL rules had created a gap in justice.

Exact returned source excerpt

Individuals most in need of legal services, especially those with low incomes, were unable to access them because of the monopoly prices.

Exact returned source excerpt

Corpus synthesis of this source: 2024 Formal Ethics Opinion 1 — Use of Artificial Intelligence in a Law Practice permits lawyers to use AI subject to professional safeguards. It recognises efficiency and consistency benefits but warns that apparently reliable outputs can be inaccurate. The supplied material offers ethical guidance and background references, not measured performance evidence.

Corpus source-specific finding

Lawyers may use AI only with competence, confidentiality protections and proper supervision.

Exact returned source excerpt

**Opinion #1:** Yes, provided the lawyer uses any AI program, tool, or resource competently, securely to protect client confidentiality, and with proper supervision when relying upon or implementing the AI’s work product in the provision of legal services.

Corpus source-specific finding

Efficiency and consistency benefits do not establish output reliability.

Exact returned source excerpt

Within the year preceding the date of this opinion, generative AI programs that *create* products in response to a user’s request based upon a large set of existing data upon which the program has been trained (e.g., Chat-GPT) have grown in capability and popularity, generating both positive and negative reactions regarding the integration of these technological breakthroughs in the legal profession.3 It is unquestioned that AI can be used in the practice of law to increase efficiency and consistency in the provision of legal services.

Exact returned source excerpt

However, AI and its work product can be inaccurate or unreliable despite its appearance of reliability when used during the provision of legal services.4  **Inquiry #1:** Considering the advantages and disadvantages of using AI in the provision of legal services, is a lawyer permitted to use AI in a law practice?

Exact returned source excerpt

AI encompasses, but is not limited to, both extractive and generative AI,1 natural language processing, large language models, and any number of machine learning processes.2 Examples of law-related AI programs range from online electronic legal research and case management software to e-discovery tools and programs that draft legal documents (e.g., a trial brief, will, etc.) based upon the lawyer’s input of information that may or may not be client-specific.

Exact returned source excerpt

Most lawyers have likely used some form of AI when practicing law, even if they didn’t realize it (e.g., widely used online legal research subscription services utilize a type of extractive AI, or a program that “extracts” information relevant to the user’s inquiry from a large set of existing data upon which the program has been trained).

Indications that work may have been produced by AI: • References to cases that do not sound familiar, or have unfamiliar citations (sometimes from the US), • Parties citing different bodies of case law in relation to the same legal issues, • Submissions that do not accord with your general understanding of the law in the area, • Submissions that use American spelling or refer to overseas cases, • Content that (superficially at least) appears to be highly persuasive and well written, but on closer inspection contains obvious substantive errors, and • The accidental inclusion of an AI prompt, or the retention of a ‘prompt rejection’, such as “as an AI language model, I can’t …” 7

SupportsCan Robot Lawyers Close the Access to Justice Gap?

Can Robot Lawyers Close the Access to Justice Gap?

Regulators, courts & professional bodies · Generative shock (2023-2024)

Corpus framing of this source: Generative AI’s access-to-justice promise requires reliability safeguards and careful UPL guidance

Corpus synthesis of this source: In their December 2024 Colorado Lawyer article, Maria E. Berkenkotter and Lino S. Lipinsky de Orlov argue that inexpensive AI could help people who cannot obtain counsel navigate civil justice. They illustrate emerging uses, distinguish reported capabilities from uncertain lawyer replacement, and examine reliability, confidentiality, inequality and unauthorized-practice risks. They recommend education and regulatory guidance that protects litigants without suppressing useful innovation.

Corpus source-specific finding

Severe unmet civil legal need motivates the case for AI assistance.

Exact returned source excerpt

Americans with a household income of $25,000 or less see complete resolution of their legal problems at a rate of only 44%.17 Recent statistics in Colorado are equally grim: last year, roughly 98% of defendants in county court civil cases, nearly 40% of district court civil litigants in cases outside family law, and about 75% of parties in domestic relations cases did not have lawyers.18 For almost all pro se litigants, this is not a matter of choice.

Exact returned source excerpt

Rather, most people who represent themselves in the Colorado courts can neither afford a lawyer nor obtain representation from Colorado Legal Services or a pro bono lawyer.19 ## The Revolution Has Begun Until recently, the powerful AI tools in widespread use today were the stuff of science fiction.

Corpus source-specific finding

Conversational AI can make legal information and procedural assistance more accessible.

Exact returned source excerpt

Now, a generative AI tool can answer questions in seconds on seemingly any topic, draft text in prose or poetry, mimic the style of famed authors, generate images and videos, and even engage in conversation.20 Generative AI resources such as ChatGPT-4, a state-of-the-art generative AI tool, are trained using large language models (LLM) to recognize and generate text.21 LLMs use massive data sets that, through a form of machine learning known as deep learning, teach the program how characters, words, and sentences function together.22 When training a deep learning model algorithm using huge volumes of data, the algorithm performs and evaluates millions of exercises to predict the next element in a sequence.23 The training creates a neural network of parameters that can generate content, such as words, images, and a command in a line of code, autonomously in response to prompts.

Exact returned source excerpt

With AI’s astonishing speed and user-friendly interfaces that move far beyond traditional internet search tools, nonlawyers can now prompt a “conversation” with a chatbot to ask any number of legal questions‍—‍from how to file a small claims case in Denver or Grand Junction or Lamar to how to defend an eviction action.

Corpus source-specific finding

Emerging products illustrate practical assistance, but lawyer replacement remains tentative.

Exact returned source excerpt

Some of these apps may‍—‍perhaps‍—‍even replace lawyers.39 The prospect of “AI giving legal advice [is] very real.”40 For example, the Colorado Legal Services website links to “Divorce Pro,” an AI-powered online tool that Professor Lois Lupica and her team at the University of Denver Sturm College of Law developed for self-represented parties in uncontested dissolution of marriage cases.41 In addition, an engineer and a librarian founded Courtroom5, a company that offers an AI tool to assist pro se litigants with their legal matters.

Exact returned source excerpt

According to the company’s website, the tool “uses simple AI to assess patterns in past cases and recommend next steps in a user’s own case, including filing documents, making a counterclaim or challenging the case entirely.”42 The company reports that self-represented parties have used Courtroom5 “in cases of home foreclosure, medical debt and difficult divorce.”43 Courtroom5 has also sought to expand its services, reporting that it was working on “a generative AI chatbot based on anonymous case records held in its own database.”44 Courtroom5 claims to be available in all 50 states.

Corpus source-specific finding

Fluency and legal specialization do not establish reliability.

Exact returned source excerpt

Between 2014 and 2017, Amazon attempted to build a tool that would rate top job candidates.33 Its programmers eventually realized, however, that the tool disproportionately preferred male applicants.

Exact returned source excerpt

It even penalized applications that referenced “women.”34 This, they learned, was because the algorithm was trained with Amazon applicant data from the prior decade, when the applicants were disproportionately male.35 Further, generative AI tools intended for the general public are not currently trained with data sets containing comprehensive, accurate legal resources.

Corpus source-specific finding

Nonlawyers may struggle to verify output, increasing risks to litigants and courts.

Exact returned source excerpt

How many will grasp that, when asked a legal question, generative AI is likely to respond‍ ‍quite confidently‍, ‍even if the answer contains errors, hallucinations, falsehoods, or biases?48 The lawyers for the plaintiff in *Mata v.

Exact returned source excerpt

Avianca Inc.* were the first to be sanctioned for filing a brief drafted by generative AI that contained “hallucinated” case citations and quotations.49 They, unfortunately, were not the last.

Corpus source-specific finding

AI could also widen the justice gap through unequal access or reduced support for counsel.

Exact returned source excerpt

Paradoxically, some of the AI tools developed with the goal of expanding access to justice carry a risk of narrowing it.

Exact returned source excerpt

Experts cite three specific examples of how AI could pose barriers to justice: (1) the cost of obtaining and implementing high-quality AI may be so high that only high-income individuals or wealthy law firms can access it, presenting an asymmetry in power between firms and individuals; (2) low-income individuals may lack the communication tools to access AI in the first place; and (3) the progression of AI may disincentivize lawmakers from enacting legislation concerning the right to counsel in civil cases.

Corpus source-specific finding

UPL guidance should distinguish tool types and protect useful innovation.

Exact returned source excerpt

Robots are not lawyers, and they are definitely not licensed to practice law (at least not yet), and AI is not infallible‍—‍even when used by lawyers.

Exact returned source excerpt

For this reason, the legal profession needs to draw a distinction between AI tools that are designed to assist individuals with legal matters and general AI tools that are not trained to provide responses regarding the law.
SupportsCan Robots Be Lawyers? Computers, Lawyers, and the Practice of Law

Can Robots Be Lawyers? Computers, Lawyers, and the Practice of Law

Academia & research · Automation debate (2015-2022)

Corpus framing of this source: Legal AI advances through structured tasks, not unrestricted lawyering

Corpus synthesis of this source: Can Robots Be Lawyers? Computers, Lawyers, and the Practice of Law examines displacement claims against the capabilities and limits of legal AI at the time of writing. The authors describe meaningful automation, explain barriers to unstructured communication, and predict further progress in structured work rather than establish imminent profession-wide replacement.

Corpus source-specific finding

Existing applications challenge the claim that lawyering is irreducibly human.

Exact returned source excerpt

machine learning application that automates document classification in discovery practice.3 Ross Intelligence, a legal application of IBM’s Watson, advertises the ability to provide concise answers to natural language legal questions.4 LegalZoom, RocketLawyer, and other online legal service providers produce basic wills, divorce agreements, contracts and incorporation papers without a lawyer’s involvement.5 These technologies challenge the traditionalist view that lawyering is irreducibly human, and force us to recognize that computers are changing the way law is practiced.

Corpus source-specific finding

The authors frame broad displacement as a possibility to examine, not an established outcome.

Exact returned source excerpt

The internet, email, and legal research databases like Westlaw and Lexis have been impacting and altering legal practice for decades.6 But from another perspective, we may be on the precipice of a more fundamental shift.

Exact returned source excerpt

Machine learning applications appear poised to displace lawyers, to make inroads on the profession’s monopoly, and to open new ways of addressing the access to justice gap.

Corpus source-specific finding

Progress concentrates in narrow tasks with trainable contingencies, and applications differ in maturity.

Exact returned source excerpt

Electronic copy available at: https://ssrn.com/abstract=2701092 ^Lrelatively narrow tasks—tasks where it is feasible to train the model on most of the contingencies it is likely to confront (though this training may occur over time as the model is used.131 These restrictions are apparent in the progress of legal artificial intelligence to date: major inroads in document classification in discovery (the subject of Markoff’s original article) and developing inroads in organizing, drafting, and reviewing contracts for due diligence.

Exact returned source excerpt

Other applications are at more embryonic stages: question answering systems using highly trained data bases; improved search and information extraction tools to locate particular information within the enterprise or within a particular body of documents; data-based tools for cost and risk analysis and prediction; and expert systems that offer a platform to provide pre-packaged legal advice.

Corpus source-specific finding

Document comparison avoids, rather than solves, the difficulty of understanding unstructured communication.

Exact returned source excerpt

With the exception of expert systems, these applications all involve language processing —in particular, measuring the similarity of meaning between two documents (or pieces of text).

Exact returned source excerpt

By focusing on comparisons between two documents, the software sidesteps problems in inferring meaning in less structured situations—for example, interpreting a client letter that uses common sense reasoning as part of its language.132 The difficulty in solving these problems helps to explain why artificial intelligence has not, to this point, penetrated those lawyer’s tasks that require unstructured communication.

Corpus source-specific finding

The authors predict continued expansion within structured legal work despite these limitations.

Exact returned source excerpt

Despite these limitations, artificial intelligence will both extend existing applications and address other, structured tasks.

Corpus synthesis of this source: Ethics of AI in Law: Basic Questions offers a role-based ethical framework and a conditional argument about how superior AI-assisted prediction could change lawyers’ duties and deepen resource inequalities. Exact returned source excerpt

It is helpful to conceptually divide the users of AI within law into three groups: the administrators of law (e.g., judges, legislators, police), the practitioners of law (e.g., attorneys), and the users of law (e.g., ordinary citizens and businesses—those who comply with the law).

Exact returned source excerpt

This concep- tual division is useful, because each group plays a different role in the legal system, and each uses AI within law in different ways.

lawyers have begun to use AI systems in a number of settings that were traditionally in the purview of legal judgment, including automated document analysis, automated ­discovery, and in legal case outcome prediction.

SupportsFighting the Hypothetical: Why Law Firms Should Rethink the Billable Hour in the Generative AI Era

Fighting the Hypothetical: Why Law Firms Should Rethink the Billable Hour in the Generative AI Era

Academia & research · Adoption at scale (2025)

Corpus framing of this source: Rethinking legal fees, staffing and service delivery as GenAI scales legal work

Corpus synthesis of this source: Fighting the Hypothetical: Why Law Firms Should Rethink the Billable Hour in the Generative AI Era argues that GenAI makes routine-work pricing and human-only delivery increasingly difficult to sustain. The authors recommend automation, client savings and greater emphasis on human expertise, while acknowledging judgment limits, ethical risks and training challenges. They support their argument with published estimates, surveys and interviews whose forecasts remain uncertain.

Corpus source-specific finding

Human-only delivery creates scalability and financial pressures that the authors expect GenAI to relieve.

Exact returned source excerpt

Indeed, when only humans are delivering legal services, scaling up and scaling down based on variable client demand requires a choice between instantaneous hiring and firing or keeping fully compensated, unproductive bodies on the bench.

Exact returned source excerpt

As a close relative of the scalability deficiency, law firms are also challenged by a financial structure with significant fixed expenses and a highly variable revenue stream.

Corpus source-specific finding

The authors use automation estimates and task-performance evidence to argue for delegating routine work, not all legal judgment.

Exact returned source excerpt

One Goldman Sachs[ ] analysis determined that about 44% of legal services could be subject to automation by GenAI systems while the remaining amount can be supplemented by GenAI.

Exact returned source excerpt

This figure is only second to the figure for office and administrative support (46% of this work may be replaced by AI) and significantly higher than the figure for all industries (25%).

Corpus source-specific finding

GenAI may augment experienced lawyers’ decisions as well as automate tasks.

Exact returned source excerpt

By analyzing historical case data, predicting litigation outcomes, and identifying patterns in judicial decisions, GenAI provides lawyers with unprecedented insights.101 Clients used to be willing to pay for a senior lawyer’s experience and judgment when applying the law to a fact pattern.

Exact returned source excerpt

Now, the use of big data can be a substitute for some of that experience, or at least an addition to it.

Corpus source-specific finding

Client pressure makes pricing reform both a competitive and an ethical issue.

Exact returned source excerpt

According to a survey by LexisNexis Legal & Professional, roughly 60% of corporate counsel across several major countries expect their outside law firms to use generative AI tools in their work.102 The combination of big data and GenAI has become a proxy for a lawyer’s judgment, augmenting and validating it with objective data.

Exact returned source excerpt

And client demands are pushing law firms to explore alternative fee arrangements, create productized legal services, and even charge for services in subscription-based models.

Corpus source-specific finding

Interviewees foresee uneven, externally driven change rather than a uniform revolution.

Exact returned source excerpt

There was consensus among our interviewees that significant disruption is coming, though they disagreed about the timeline.

Exact returned source excerpt

Many predict that mid-tier and regional firms may face the greatest disruption, while elite firms are likely to maintain their position through specialized expertise and judgment- focused work.

Corpus source-specific finding

Automation threatens traditional junior development but may create opportunities in underserved markets.

Exact returned source excerpt

Corpus framing of this source: Joint Formal Opinion 2024-200: Applying existing ethical duties to AI use

Corpus synthesis of this source: Joint Formal Opinion 2024-200 — Ethical Issues Regarding the Use of Artificial Intelligence treats generative AI as a significant technological development governed by existing professional duties. It sets out requirements for competence, verification, confidentiality, client communication and expenses, illustrates risks of misplaced confidence and bias, and reports court-specific disclosure and certification requirements.

Corpus source-specific finding

Generative AI changes legal capabilities, but existing ethical rules remain the governing framework.

Exact returned source excerpt

Generative Artificial Intelligence Although artificial intelligence has been used for decades, generative AI represents a significant change and a dramatic step forward in legal applications, because instead of only analyzing content, it can also generate new content.

Exact returned source excerpt

As with other forms of technology, from cloud computing to virtual offices, these new technologies implicate old ethical problems.

Corpus source-specific finding

Technological competence requires proficiency and continuing education.

Exact returned source excerpt

The Committees also emphasize that lawyers must be proficient in using technological tools to the same extent they are in employing traditional methods.

Exact returned source excerpt

Whether it is understanding how to navigate legal research databases, use e-discovery software, use their smartphones, use email, or otherwise safeguard client information in digital formats, lawyers are required to maintain competence across all technological means relevant to their practice.

Corpus source-specific finding

Lawyers must verify AI-assisted legal work rather than rely on apparent plausibility.

Exact returned source excerpt

As outlined in more detail in the “Guidance & Best Practices for the Use of Artificial Intelligence” section below, this Joint Opinion is intended to educate attorneys on the benefits and pitfalls of using this type of technology, and provide ethical guidelines, including: 1 ^L • Lawyers must ensure that AI-generated content, such as legal documents or advice, is truthful, accurate, and based on sound legal reasoning, upholding principles of honesty and integrity in their professional conduct.

Exact returned source excerpt

• Lawyers must ensure the accuracy and relevance of the citations they use in legal documents or arguments.

Corpus source-specific finding

AI use must preserve client protections and transparent charging.

Exact returned source excerpt

• Lawyers must safeguard information relating to the representation of a client and ensure that AI systems handling confidential data adhere to strict confidentiality measures.

Exact returned source excerpt

• Lawyers must be vigilant in identifying and addressing potential conflicts of interest arising from using AI systems.

Corpus source-specific finding

Predictive risk assessments can perpetuate systemic bias.

Exact returned source excerpt

If the algorithm disproportionately flags individuals from marginalized communities as high-risk, it could lead to unjust outcomes such as harsher sentences, perpetuating systemic biases within the legal system.

Corpus source-specific finding

Some courts impose their own AI disclosure and verification requirements.

Exact returned source excerpt

Exact returned source excerpt

For example, one federal judge in the Eastern District of Pennsylvania has issued a standing order requiring: … that counsel (or a party representing himself or herself) disclose whether he or she has used generative Artificial Intelligence (“AI”) in the preparation of any complaint, answer, motion, brief, or other paper filed with the Court, including in correspondence with the Court.

Exact returned source excerpt

Exact returned source excerpt

Exact returned source excerpt

AI is a broad term that encompasses various pieces of technology that mimic humanlike behavior.[[16]](#fn16) According to The National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST), systems that use AI are adaptive.[[17]](#fn17) They can solve problems, make predictions, and complete tasks that require cognition and other human-like senses.[[18]](#fn18) AI systems work with machine learning (ML) and data analytics to make intelligent decisions and form conclusions using real-time data.[[19]](#fn19) However, in order to make intelligent decisions, computer programmers must build intelligent algorithms.[[20]](#fn20) ML and natural language processing (NLP) are subfields of AI.[[21]](#fn21) ML systems teach algorithms to interpret new data without relying on rules-based programming, and are broken down into deep learning, supervised learning, and unsupervised learning.[[22]](#fn22) NLP systems can extract content, classify information, machine translate, answer questions, and generate text.[[23]](#fn23) GPT is an acronym for “generative pre-training transformer.”[[24]](#fn24) The GPT system is an autoregressive language processing model.

Exact returned source excerpt

[36]](#fn36) For example, GPT-4 completed the Uniform Bar Exam, LSAT, GRE, and various AP tests.[[37]](#fn37) GPT-4 exhibited “human-level performance” on these exams and scored in the top 10% of Uniform Bar Exam test takers.[[38]](#fn38) In his article, David Becerra offers the following working definition of AI in the legal field: “[T]he theory and development of processes performed by software instead of a legal practitioner, whose outcome is the same as if a legal practitioner had done the work.”[[39]](#fn39) This definition considers both the technology and the legal task AI is meant to perform.[[40]](#fn40) The comprehensive scope of this definition, therefore, includes the various different pieces of AI and generative AI technology used in the legal field.[[41]](#fn41) It will be the definition that underscores the ideas discussed in this Comment.

Corpus source-specific finding

AI promises lower costs and more time for legal analysis, while surveyed respondents express substantial ethical concern.

Exact returned source excerpt

Reliance on Artificial Intelligence The benefits of using AI in the legal profession are clear.[[44]](#fn44) Law firms have used systems backed by AI because they help lawyers save time.[[45]](#fn45) Instead of working on routine and mundane tasks, lawyers direct their attention to work that requires legal analysis.[[46]](#fn46) The systems quickly produce accurate and high-quality work that can be organized in a logical, coherent document.

Exact returned source excerpt

[47]](#fn47) According to a survey conducted by LexisNexis, 47% of respondents consisting of lawyers, law students, and consumers from the United States, United Kingdom, Canada, and France believe that generative AI will have a “significant or transformative” impact in the legal field.[[48]](#fn48) The potential for AI to transform the legal field, particularly by cutting costs, comes at a time when clients are continuing to demand more for less.[[49]](#fn49) Judges are noticing the potential of technology assisted review (TAR), which relies on predictive coding, to reduce court costs and allow for a more “efficient and superior [alternative] to keyword searching.”[[50]](#fn50) However, data protection and client confidentiality remain at the forefront of many legal professionals’ minds.

Corpus source-specific finding

Existing research and document tools illustrate augmentation; dramatic drafting savings remain a reported prediction.

Exact returned source excerpt

Legal Research* Legal research engines, like Westlaw and LexisNexis, incorporate elements of AI for legal research.[[53]](#fn53) Although it might seem like a recent development, Westlaw has been using components of AI for two decades.[[54]](#fn54) Starting in 2003, Westlaw used AI to better understand what researchers were looking for and provide more targeted search results.[[55]](#fn55) Similarly, Lexis uses AI to give researchers more accurate answers and analyze briefs.[[56]](#fn56) The advances in AI allow lawyers to quickly access cases that will be most helpful to their arguments.[[57]](#fn57) #### *2.

Exact returned source excerpt

Young associates no longer need to spend hours reviewing large amounts of data and documents.[[58]](#fn58) AI can quickly “aggregate data and match a finite set of outcomes to the answers to questions.”[[59]](#fn59) For example, ML software systems, such as Kira, are able to identify and analyze information in contracts and documents.[[60]](#fn60) Law firms use Kira to assist with due diligence because it can pull clauses and provisions found in contracts and documents and then organize the information in different graphs, tables, and reports.[[61]](#fn61) In addition to Kira, systems like Litera Check use ML to proofread multiple documents and streamline the document review process.[[62]](#fn62) In addition to document review, young associates can use generative AI systems, such as ChatGPT, to compose initial drafts of contracts and motions.[[63]](#fn63) Experts believe that using generative AI systems will reduce the amount of time worked from ten hours to fifteen minutes and increase overall productivity.[[64]](#fn64) #### *3.

Corpus source-specific finding

Predictive coding offers discovery benefits but leaves ethical questions unresolved.

Exact returned source excerpt

E-Discovery* E-discovery is “[t]he process of identifying, preserving, collecting, preparing, reviewing, and producing electronically stored information.”[[65]](#fn65) E-discovery can also be referred to as TAR.[[66]](#fn66) AI technology allowed e-discovery systems, which historically focused on searching a database using keywords, to move to predictive coding.[[67]](#fn67) The switch to predictive coding provides more accurate results as to whether a document will be relevant.[[68]](#fn68) The legal community has welcomed the use of this tool.[[69]](#fn69) However, some legal commentators have warned about the costs that come with the benefits of using it, and ethical questions regarding e-discovery’s reliance on predicative coding are left unanswered.

Exact returned source excerpt

[47]](#fn47) According to a survey conducted by LexisNexis, 47% of respondents consisting of lawyers, law students, and consumers from the United States, United Kingdom, Canada, and France believe that generative AI will have a “significant or transformative” impact in the legal field.[[48]](#fn48) The potential for AI to transform the legal field, particularly by cutting costs, comes at a time when clients are continuing to demand more for less.[[49]](#fn49) Judges are noticing the potential of technology assisted review (TAR), which relies on predictive coding, to reduce court costs and allow for a more “efficient and superior [alternative] to keyword searching.”[[50]](#fn50) However, data protection and client confidentiality remain at the forefront of many legal professionals’ minds.

Bucher predicts a shift toward expertise and relationships while reporting competing expectations about displacement.

is Coming for Lawyers, Again.”[[1]](#fn1) In the article, a *Times* technology reporter described how Artificial Intelligence (AI) has made lawyers’ jobs more efficient and productive.[[2]](#fn2) However, with the creation of ChatGPT and other systems using large language models, the legal profession is facing a new attack.[[3]](#fn3) This is in large part due to the ability of the new technology to predict, analyze, and produce writing—the “bread and butter”—of a lawyer’s work.[[4]](#fn4) Everyone from paralegals to highly paid partners will be required to consider how to make themselves competitive and stay ahead of the advances in technology.[[5]](#fn5) In other words, instead of focusing on work that can be done by AI, a lawyer’s job will focus on industry expertise, client relationships, and insightful guidance.[[6]](#fn6)

Corpus framing of this source: Legal AI requires task-specific safeguards, lawyer verification and protection of judicial deliberation

Corpus synthesis of this source: Tasks and Roles in Legal AI: Data Curation, Annotation, and Verification assesses AI through a user-by-task framework. They balance potential productivity and access-to-justice gains against high-stakes errors and weakened trust, while calling for interdisciplinary collaboration and open materials.

The technical ability of AI to render judicial decisions, as though a judge, is different from the technical ability of AI to summarize documents for a lawyer, as though it were an unbarred summer associate.

generating text and analysis.3 Rather than a single invention, AI encompasses a wide range of technologies, from narrow tools built for specific functions, to machine-learning systems that continually refine their performance using data, to large generative models capable of producing text, summaries, images, or speech that resemble human outputs.4 Many of these systems already permeate everyday life, for example speech-recognition tools translate voice into accurate text, and generative models prepare summaries or textual drafts in a fraction of the time human effort would require.

Builds onAccess to A.I. Justice: Avoiding an Inequitable Two-Tiered System of Legal Services

Access to A.I. Justice: Avoiding an Inequitable Two-Tiered System of Legal Services

Academia & research · Automation debate (2015-2022)

Corpus framing of this source: Calibrating legal AI to expand access without creating inferior tiers of service

Corpus synthesis of this source: Drew Simshaw’s 2022 article synthesises legal-technology scholarship and access-to-justice evidence to argue for context-specific AI use and regulatory reform. Its central contribution links equitable access to stakeholders’ ability to design and calibrate tools for particular consumers, legal issues and processes—not simply to technology adoption or falling prices.

Corpus source-specific finding

The justice gap requires a broader response than access to courts alone.

Exact returned source excerpt

In 2017, only fourteen percent of low-income Americans received adequate legal attention to the legal problems they reported.27 In 2021, a nationwide survey found that there were only 10,479 civil legal aid attorneys in the U.S., equaling just over one for every 10,000 people whose incomes fall below 200% of the federal poverty level.28 The goal of increasing “access to justice” emerged in the legal aid context in the mid-twentieth century.29 It has since often been associated with mere access to courts,30 but the term’s meaning 27 See LEGAL SERVS.

Exact returned source excerpt

Corpus source-specific finding

AI and broader legal technology can expand service capacity and reach excluded consumers.

Exact returned source excerpt

Legal AI is a subset of broader “legal technology”; this Article discusses both, because the ways in which legal technologies are being aided by AI are rapidly evolving, and this Article aims to address the opportunities and challenges presented by both current and future data- driven tools.

Exact returned source excerpt

Individuals seeking help navigating the legal system can access free guides from nonprofit organizations online.1 Legal services organizations can automate their intake to quickly direct clients to the most relevant and helpful resources for their issues,2 or even automatically generate a legal document for them.3 These services can also help consumers recognize when a legal issue requires a licensed legal professional, and can help connect those consumers with appropriate legal service providers.4 For issues that require licensed legal professionals, technology, broadly speaking, is streamlining and fundamentally changing how law is practiced,5 with law firms 1 See Raymond H.

Corpus source-specific finding

The literature identifies three distinct ways AI could preserve or deepen unequal access.

Exact returned source excerpt

But some fear that increased reliance on AI will lead to one or more two-tiered systems: the poor might be stuck with inferior AI-driven assistance; only expensive law firms might be able to effectively harness legal AI; or, AI’s impact might not disrupt the status quo where only some can afford any type of legal assistance.

Exact returned source excerpt

Underperformance and failure of technology in the pursuit of access to justice are not uncommon.122 As many scholars have noted, AI is not yet poised to deliver on many expectations, including concerning the development of general AI,123 more widespread automation,124 the ability to effectively utilize the explosion of new data,125 the ability to assist with complex tasks,126 and the ability of AI to translate into net increased efficiency for users.127 There are also technical limits on what legal tasks can even be automated in the first place.128 122 See Staudt, supra note 58, at 1122 (“Overheated expectations and early unbridled enthusiasm for breaking technologies have contributed to disappointment when projects in law and information technology produced only modest improvement or even resulted in failure.”).

Corpus source-specific finding

Appropriate AI reliance depends on the consumer, issue and process, rather than a binary choice between humans and machines.

Exact returned source excerpt

Part III provides a taxonomy of important considerations that stakeholders face when working to “calibrate” an appropriate level of AI use in light of the specific consumers, legal issues, and underlying processes involved, and argues that this calibration is key to avoiding a two-tiered system.

Exact returned source excerpt

Justice 198 American Bar Association changed the title of its Model Rule 5.3 from “Responsibilities Regarding Nonlawyer Assistants,” referring to people, to “Responsibilities Regarding Nonlawyer Assistance,”221 more broadly encompassing third party, cloud- based technology services such as AI-driven tools.222 But decisions concerning AI’s role in any given process are not always binary—reliance should have varying degrees.

Corpus source-specific finding

Exact returned source excerpt

Machines can access and process data in ways that humans simply cannot.208 For example, lawyers are increasingly reliant on eDiscovery as a process because it is usually not only faster, but also more accurate than discovery performed by humans.209 Similarly, when natural language processing is employed, machines can arrive at better search results and complete certain legal forms more accurately than humans,210 who are prone to human error.

Corpus source-specific finding

Exact returned source excerpt

Exact returned source excerpt

Although AI is often touted for its ability to lower costs for legal service providers and consumers, the technology itself is often not cheap.247 As a result, financial resources often present a major barrier to not only committing to adopt AI in the first place, but also to engaging in its calibration and ultimately realizing the benefits of the broader “AI revolution.”248 245 See, e.g., Staudt, supra note 58.

Builds onArtificial Access to Justice: AI and the Surge in Pro Se Litigation

Artificial Access to Justice: AI and the Surge in Pro Se Litigation

Academia & research · Current wave (2026)

Corpus framing of this source: AI expands access to federal court without improving access to remedy

Corpus synthesis of this source: Or Cohen-Sasson’s 2026 working paper combines filing data, complaint-level analysis and doctrinal argument to describe a litigation-efficacy paradox: AI-associated pro se complaints look more professional but achieve no better outcomes. The author attributes this gap to weakened pre-filing screening and courts’ assumptions about human authorship, and recommends institutional repair rather than AI prohibition.

Corpus source-specific finding

Federal pro se filings rose sharply after ChatGPT’s release.

Exact returned source excerpt

Drawing on 2.8 million filings from 2008 through 2025 and nearly 13,000 linked pro se complaints, this Article documents the surge and reveals a paradox.

Exact returned source excerpt

3 ^Lthrough 2025 and a linked corpus of nearly 13,000 pro se complaints, the Article documents a sharp break from that baseline, and a paradox at its core.7 After ChatGPT’s public release in late 2022, the pro se share rose at an accelerating pace—from 11.3% of filings before to 22.3% by the final quarter of fiscal 2025.8 A meaningful share of the new complaints carry the textual signature of machine drafting, and those complaints look far more like professional work product than pro se filings historically have, with substantially higher citation density.9 And yet they fare worse.

Corpus source-specific finding

More professional-looking complaints do not produce better litigation outcomes.

Exact returned source excerpt

3 ^Lthrough 2025 and a linked corpus of nearly 13,000 pro se complaints, the Article documents a sharp break from that baseline, and a paradox at its core.7 After ChatGPT’s public release in late 2022, the pro se share rose at an accelerating pace—from 11.3% of filings before to 22.3% by the final quarter of fiscal 2025.8 A meaningful share of the new complaints carry the textual signature of machine drafting, and those complaints look far more like professional work product than pro se filings historically have, with substantially higher citation density.9 And yet they fare worse.

Exact returned source excerpt

AI-flagged complaints are dismissed more often, terminate earlier, and prevail no more frequently than their unassisted counterparts.10 Accordingly, while at first sight one may celebrate AI as an enabler of better access to justice, the result is not that access is simply achieved, but that access is transformed: while there is more access to courts, and more visible AI assistance, and more legal form, there is no greater access to remedy.

Corpus source-specific finding

Access to a forum and access to relief require separate assessment.

Exact returned source excerpt

Establishing that the two elements of access to justice have in fact diverged, namely, that formal entry expanded while substantive success has not, requires evidence of each, gathered separately.

Exact returned source excerpt

A long line of procedural-justice scholarship finds that people care about the dignitary value of participating in the processes that decide their disputes, and that these process values are distinct from, and not reducible to, winning.54 A complaint that is docketed and then dismissed leaves the litigant in a different position than a grievance that never reaches a court at all; the first was heard, the second was not.

Corpus source-specific finding

AI shifts screening from before filing onto the court docket.

Exact returned source excerpt

Between a grievance and a filed complaint, two filters historically existed, a friction filter and a professional-judgment filter.

Exact returned source excerpt

First, at the threshold, AI dissolves the drafting friction that once screened filings and replaces lawyers’ candid “no” to claims not worth bringing with an AI advisor inclined to validate them.

Corpus source-specific finding

AI authorship disrupts pleading doctrines and can create a polish penalty.

Exact returned source excerpt

Second, once a complaint is filed, courts apply doctrines that assume one person signed the complaint, knows its facts, and chose its words; AI use disrupts that assumption.

Exact returned source excerpt

Plausibility pleading, Rule 11, and liberal construction each misfire when the author is, in part, a machine.

Corpus source-specific finding

Repair institutional functions rather than suppress AI assistance.

Exact returned source excerpt

That burden has prompted tool-centric responses, from disclosure to prohibition.

Exact returned source excerpt

The emerging proposals regulate the use of the tool; this Article shows why that cannot work, and builds the alternative: a function-centric program that rebuilds screening, comprehension, and solicitude on the court’s side without withdrawing the access AI has produced.44 The Article proceeds in five Parts.

Corpus source-specific finding

Other doctrines may also require scrutiny of assumptions about cost and human authorship.

Exact returned source excerpt

The pro se complaint is one place where that has now happened; it is unlikely to be the only one.

Exact returned source excerpt

Wherever a rule leans, however quietly, on the cost of some legal act or on the human authorship of legal documents, the arrival of capable machines invites the question this Article has put to pleading, candor, and solicitude: what was the rule actually assuming, and does the assumption still hold?

5-Apr-26] AI and Human Legal Reasoning 13 judicial opinions,35 it can be mitigated through long standing and routine lawyering practices, such as verifying the existence, accuracy, and relevance of cited authorities in final versions of submitted materials.36 Additionally, legal AI providers are aware of this concern and have invested in models specifically trained to facilitate and even automate cite-checking.37 Nonetheless, AI may exacerbate the risk that attorneys will misread cases, overstate holdings, or cite authorities that do not squarely support their claims—particularly when using AI to generate legal documents quickly.

Exact returned source excerpt

225 Other states have since followed suit by licensing paralegals and community justice workers— nonlawyers who are trained by not-for-profit organizations — to provide legal services directly to the public.226 Court navigator programs are also prevalent, although navigators are usually supervised and do not provide the same spectrum of services.227 The current era provides an opportunity to consider whether there should be greater role differentiation between lawyer and nonlawyer providers.

Corpus source-specific finding

Require verifiable factual and legal claims in high-volume proceedings, regardless of whether an AI litigation boom occurs.

Exact returned source excerpt

Second, courts should bolster existing ethical requirements and mandate the reasonable verification of factual claims and legal authorities in proceedings that commonly involve unrepresented parties.

Exact returned source excerpt

Courts should mandate certification in cases involving foreclosure, debt collection, and eviction even in the absence of the anticipated AI litigation boom.289 These cases have overwhelmed state courts in recent decades and are rarely subjected to adversarial scrutiny.290 The advent of AI is poised to exacerbate the situation, potentially elevating lack of access to justice to an existential crisis for the legal system itself.

Generative AI Use in the Legal Profession: Findings from the 2025 Victorian Lawyer Census

Regulators, courts & professional bodies · Current wave (2026)

Corpus framing of this source: Victorian lawyer census: uneven AI adoption, governance gaps and unrealised client benefits

Corpus synthesis of this source: Generative AI Use in the Legal Profession: Findings from the 2025 Victorian Lawyer Census reports lawyers’ adoption, safeguards and perceptions, rather than measured client outcomes. The VLSB+C report combines these findings with earlier research to question whether efficiency gains will improve legal services and recommends targeted support for safe, equitable implementation.

Corpus source-specific finding

Low-cost general-purpose AI broadens access to technology but introduces professional risks.

Exact returned source excerpt

Emerging from an original focus on expert and case-based reasoning systems, the last 20 years has seen greater attention directed towards the development of specific machine-learning and natural language processing-powered tools.1 This has given rise to a range of products intended to accelerate litigation discovery processes, to scale contract analysis, review and production, to enable natural language query-based search, and to support litigation analytics.2 These tools have typically been developed for, and adopted within, large commercial firms working with corporate or financial entities.

Exact returned source excerpt

Recognising the potential of these underlying technologies, established legal technology vendors have rapidly developed domain-specific applications that leverage large language models while constraining them to vetted legal libraries and resources.4 But unlike these domain-specific applications, general-purpose large language models, such as ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini and Perplexity, offer capabilities that extend across multiple professional domains, including law.

Corpus source-specific finding

Experimental evidence supports faster work, not consistent quality improvement.

Exact returned source excerpt

Experimental work suggests these tools can deliver efficiency gains, with studies finding that law students using GPT-4 completed legal analysis tasks significantly faster than those working without AI assistance.5 Yet the evidence on quality improvements remains mixed.

Exact returned source excerpt

The same study found that while lower-skilled participants saw some quality improvements on certain tasks, these gains were neither consistent nor universal.

Corpus source-specific finding

Adoption centres on general-purpose tools and information, drafting and administrative tasks.

Exact returned source excerpt

In light of this, the 2025 Victorian Lawyer Census included a new suite of questions designed to capture AI use among lawyers and understand the factors shaping adoption decisions.

Exact returned source excerpt

Just over half agreed that AI is a necessary element of modern legal practice, despite only just over a third currently using it.

Corpus source-specific finding

Privacy and governance concerns impede adoption; most users report verification.

Exact returned source excerpt

Strong endorsement of professional duties coexists with limited guidance awareness.

Exact returned source excerpt

Exact returned source excerpt

greatest interest in training on using AI to enhance workflow and efficiency, and in safe and ethical use.

Corpus source-specific finding

Respondents chiefly perceive efficiency benefits, not improved client outcomes.

Exact returned source excerpt

Exact returned source excerpt

Corpus source-specific finding

Exact returned source excerpt

Exact returned source excerpt

Corpus source-specific finding

Exact returned source excerpt

Current adoption patterns reveal that AI’s benefits are both narrowly conceived and unevenly distributed.

Generative AI and the Future of the Courts: Responsibilities and Possibilities

Regulators, courts & professional bodies · Generative shock (2023-2024)

Corpus framing of this source: COSCA: problem-led AI adoption for court access, administration and staffing

Corpus synthesis of this source: Generative AI and the Future of the Courts: Responsibilities and Possibilities presents COSCA’s recommendations for using generative AI to advance equal justice and administrative efficiency. It combines predictions of substantial workplace change with examples of early court adoption, evidence of unmet legal need, and safeguards for confidentiality, staff assistance, workforce planning and pilot evaluation.

Corpus source-specific finding

Courts should choose AI around identified problems, not adopt technology for its own sake.

Exact returned source excerpt

COSCA’s 2001 position paper on effective judicial governance and accountability states this obligation simply as “modern technological innovations must be fully utilized.”29 COSCA believes that generative AI is poised to change the workplace as dramatically as the introduction of computers and case management software did in the early 1980s.

Exact returned source excerpt

Found at: https://wlr.law.wisc.edu/making-sense-of-legal-disruption/ Generative AI and the Future of Courts 15 ^LDepending on the structure of the court system, the charge of a taskforce might include developing guiding principles for the responsible use of AI 40 and recommending a general use policy41; reviewing existing privacy and security rules and policies, reviewing the rules of evidence and procedural rules, reviewing the ethical and practical considerations of expanding the court’s role in access to justice, and exploring the potential to reform court processes and the administration of the court system.42 It is tempting to start taskforce work with these two questions, “How can we best use AI?” and “How can we mitigate or minimize the risks of using AI?” A better approach is to first decide what problem a court is trying to solve, and then develop possible solutions, before turning to the question of whether there is an AI tool that will support the solution.

Corpus source-specific finding

AI could help self-represented litigants, but courts must address unequal access and additional citation-review work.

Found at: https://repository.uchastings.edu/hastings_law_ journal/vol74/iss2/3 Generative AI and the Future of Courts 9 ^LCourts Have an Obligation to Ensure A I Access is Fair According to a 2015 study conducted by NCSC, in 72% of family law cases and 76% of nonfamily law civil cases, at least one party is self-represented.23 Another study conducted by The Legal Services Corporation estimates that low-income Americans did not receive any or enough legal help for 92% of their civil legal problems.24 Those unable to afford an attorney have historically struggled to navigate the legal system and avoid procedural hurdles.

Corpus source-specific finding

Exact returned source excerpt

in Administrative Processes Courts have a fiscal responsibility to provide services in the most cost-effective manner available.

Exact returned source excerpt

Ryan Roslansky, the CEO of LinkedIn estimates that for 80% of their employees, AI will be able to automate up to 25% of their routine daily tasks.30 Instead of thinking about positions that may benefit from the use of AI, the key to determining where to use generative AI is to consider the tasks that make up individual positions and then decide which tasks should be automated through AI, augmented through AI or left as a solely human task.31 Like many employers in recent years, state courts across the country face difficulties in recruiting and retaining staff.32 The resulting efforts to maintain services with fewer employees can lead to heightened stress and burnout for those who choose to support the rule of law by working in the courts.

Exact returned source excerpt

Builds onInteroperable Legal AI for Access to Justice

Interoperable Legal AI for Access to Justice

Academia & research · Adoption at scale (2025)

Corpus framing of this source: Court-led interoperability as a condition for AI-enabled access to justice

Corpus synthesis of this source: Drew Simshaw’s 2025 essay argues that isolated improvements in consumer tools, legal services and courts cannot close the justice gap at national scale. Drawing on reported examples and scholarship rather than a new empirical evaluation, he recommends coordinated technological and procedural standards, court leadership and a national regulatory sandbox, while warning that efficiency alone does not ensure fairness.

Corpus source-specific finding

Progress across consumers, providers and courts must connect rather than proceed in isolation.

Exact returned source excerpt

This Essay argues that technological and procedural legal interoperability—that is, widespread consistency in technology design and related processes—should be at the forefront of these efforts, particularly as they relate to artificial intelligence.

Exact returned source excerpt

While progress is apparent, it is not taking place at the pace or scale necessary to make meaningful inroads into closing the justice gap nationwide.8 Access for access’s sake and efficiency for efficiency’s sake will not necessarily result in improvements to fairness, especially if AI is designed and implemented in ways that intentionally or unintentionally automate bias and magnify inequality.9 The time has come to appreciate that, although progress on all three fronts is necessary for closing the justice gap and maximizing fairness, it will be insufficient if there is not also some level of shared commitment and coordination across—and not just within—all fronts.

Corpus source-specific finding

The scale of unmet need makes local, limited initiatives insufficient.

Exact returned source excerpt

In 2023, for example, the Georgetown Law Center on Ethics and the Legal Profession concluded that closing the justice gap requires substantial investment from the industry.11 The Legal Services Corporation reported in 2022 that, despite recent efforts, “[l]ow‑income Americans do not get any or enough legal help for 92% of their substantial civil legal problems.”12 This Part analyzes the isolated progress seen to date on each front and the limitations of long-term progress absent interoperable legal AI.

Exact returned source excerpt

the limits of ai efforts with consumers, service providers, and courts The legal problem-solving landscape has made commendable efforts to leverage legal technologies to make inroads in closing the access-to-justice gap.10 But the results have been too local, are limited in scope, and lack the scalability needed to maximize impact.

Corpus source-specific finding

Self-help tools demonstrate useful applications, but examples do not establish uniformly successful outcomes.

Exact returned source excerpt

The Consumer Front In some cases, technology-driven tools are helping people solve their own legal problems—ranging from creating their own wills and trusts,13 to drafting routine legal documents,14 to completing other tasks that do not always require the help of a professional.15 Nonprofessional assistance has always been in demand,16 and AI has stepped up to help meet it.

Exact returned source excerpt

17 HelloPrenup, a service designed to help couples with prenuptial agreements, secured $150,000 in investment on the popular television show Shark Tank.18 Rasa, a technology-driven app-based service, helps people in Utah assess their eligibility for expungement of their criminal records and, if eligible, navigate the process with the help of AI-enabled software.19 And, of course, LegalZoom has become almost synonymous with legal self-help, assisting “over two million individuals and small businesses by helping consumers prepare downloadable legal documents such as wills, prenuptial agreements, copyrights, real estate leases, and articles of incorporation.”20 In a landscape of regulatory uncertainty, some of these services have resulted in mixed receptions and results.

Corpus source-specific finding

Regulatory fragmentation obstructs development and interdisciplinary collaboration.

Exact returned source excerpt

Indeed, innovation in this space is stifled by uncertainty as to whether certain tools and services constitute the unauthorized practice of law, which is defined and regulated differently across U.S.

Exact returned source excerpt

jurisdictions.26 In addition, partnerships between lawyers and technologists to develop and provide such tools are often hindered by the nearly universal prohibition in U.S.

Corpus source-specific finding

Provider-side efficiency gains may disproportionately benefit large firms and wealthy clients.

Exact returned source excerpt

The Legal-Services Front For those cases that require the help of legal professionals, technology has also made noticeable and commendable strides.29 It is now widely recognized that AI has the ability to increase the efficiency of legal tasks30 ranging from intake,31 to eDiscovery,32 to legal research,33 to developing case strategy,34 and even to assisting with drafting legal documents, though not without high-profile misuses.35 But much of this development is happening in-house at the largest corporate law firms.36 And the most impactful generative-AI developments, like Harvey AI—essentially a more dependable and tailored ChatGPT for lawyers—are designed for and marketed to large firms.37 The “two worlds” of legal-technology development were recently observed in two vastly different legal-technology conferences: a “glitzy celebration of big law tech” at Legalweek, and the very modest and understated Legal Services Corporation’s Innovations in Technology Conference, “devoted to tech for access to justice.

Exact returned source excerpt

38 Bob Ambrogi described the conferences as illustrative of the “funding gap between those who are developing legal technology to better meet the legal needs of low-income Americans and those who are developing legal tech to serve large law firms and corporate legal departments.”39 While it is possible that such services will trickle down to benefit those outside large law firms, services designed for one setting do not always translate well to others.

Corpus source-specific finding

Court digitization and data standards provide foundations, not complete interoperability.

Exact returned source excerpt

courts in recent years has been the digitization of court forms, which has facilitated electronic filing and other electronic case management.42 For self-represented litigants, many courts have also made efforts to digitize court documents, post free legal forms online for litigants, and (in fewer jurisdictions) even provide computer kiosks to help people navigate their interactions with the court.43 In civil litigation, courts have also been involved in overseeing eDiscovery practices by litigants, including by assessing whether proper search terms and coding are being used by both sides throughout the process.44 Of course, many jurisdictions appreciate that a truly efficient ecosystem is one in which technology can help prevent many cases from needing to reach the courts in the first place.

Exact returned source excerpt

45 Indeed, alternative dispute resolution—the process of settling disputes without litigation—has blossomed into *online* dispute resolution (ODR) through the use of algorithms to overcome the cost and limited availability of human mediators.46 When courts nevertheless become involved, some have also embraced ODR as an option for a wide range of processes at this stage,47 sometimes turning to private-sector-developed ODR resolution systems.48 All of these efforts have resulted in massive amounts of data, and some coordination across certain state courts is emerging in ways that are encouraging for eventual broader coordination on more complex interoperable legal AI.

Corpus source-specific finding

Courts should lead coordination despite their historical role as technology followers.

Exact returned source excerpt

Further, although the consumer and legal-services fronts remain critically important, courts should be recognized as the necessary drivers in achieving this interoperable legal AI.

Exact returned source excerpt

Finally, in Part III, this Essay argues that courts must be the drivers of interoperable legal AI, underscoring the potential for interoperable legal AI to align with broader efforts in AI governance that would both support and be supported by courts’ efforts.

Corpus source-specific finding

AI can deepen a two-tiered system through both inferior substitution and unequal access to superior tools.

Exact returned source excerpt

For example, if low-income individuals are relegated to technology-driven tools and services even when human-driven assistance would be more appropriate, it might be better than nothing, but still not as good as professional human services.70 But the opposite could also be true: legal technology could become incredibly powerful and effective, but not evenly distributed.71 Whereas large firms serving wealthy clients and corporations will have the means to integrate these technologies into their practice, small firms and solo practitioners may not have the resources, resilience, and relationships to do so.72 Moreover, some fear the AI-driven access-to-justice narrative is overhyped and will not significantly alter the status quo of today’s two-tiered system, where not everyone can access legal services.73 These two-tiered systems of inequality are not mutually exclusive.

Exact returned source excerpt

Without large-scale coordinated efforts across all three fronts to level the playing field and facilitate necessary interdisciplinary and cross-industry collaborations, legal AI risks consolidating power, automating bias, and magnifying inequality.

Corpus source-specific finding

A national regulatory sandbox would test innovation and support shared standards.

Exact returned source excerpt

It will therefore be important to analyze the prospect of interoperable legal AI within broader discussions of legal-regulatory reform, including my proposal for a national legal regulatory “sandbox”—a reform mechanism that would provide temporary safe harbors for testing innovative services and collecting data in areas of regulatory uncertainty.

Exact returned source excerpt

The proposed sandbox would promote standardization, transparency, and, ultimately, the technological and procedural interoperability necessary for AI to reach its potential as a tool to help close the access-to-justice gap and facilitate fair outcomes.

Builds onPaths Toward Access to Justice at Scale: Evaluating Theories of Change in the Civil Justice System

Paths Toward Access to Justice at Scale: Evaluating Theories of Change in the Civil Justice System

Academia & research · Current wave (2026)

Corpus framing of this source: AI as a proposed route beyond scarcity in civil legal help

Corpus synthesis of this source: Margaret Hagan proposes a technology-centered theory of change for access to justice. Drawing on interviewees’ assumptions about scarce human capacity, she argues that AI could expand legal help by changing its production, while complementing institutional reform. The supplied passages offer conditional predictions and workflow proposals rather than measured results from deployments.

Corpus source-specific finding

Hagan identifies labor scarcity as an assumption running through the interviews and existing reform theories.

Exact returned source excerpt

The Scale Problem Revisited A thread running through the interviews was the assumption that access to justice ser- vices are inherently labor-intensive and therefore inherently scarce.

Exact returned source excerpt

Every theory of change discussed in this Article operates within this scarcity constraint: the question is how to allocate limited resources more effectively, create pressure for more resources, or change institutional culture to use existing resources better.

Corpus source-specific finding

She argues that AI could change the production of legal help, not merely redistribute scarce expertise.

Exact returned source excerpt

Large language models can gener- ate plain-language legal information, help people complete court forms, triage legal Working Paper by Margaret Hagan March 5, 2026 14 ^Lproblems, draft correspondence, and walk users through multi-step procedures—at marginal costs approaching zero and at a scale that no human-staffed service can match.

Exact returned source excerpt

The question is no longer only how to distribute scarce human expertise more effectively, but whether entirely new workflows can be built that serve orders of magnitude more people than current models allow.

Corpus source-specific finding

Her proposed tenth theory conditions expansion on accurate, accessible, trustworthy tools embedded where legal problems arise.

Exact returned source excerpt

What a Technology-Centered Theory of Change Would Look Like A tenth theory of change might be stated as follows: If we build AI-powered legal help tools that are accurate, accessible, and trustworthy, and embed them in the pathways where people already encounter legal problems, then we can dramatically expand the number of people who receive meaningful help—shifting the field from scarcity-driven triage to abundance-oriented service design.

Exact returned source excerpt

The other theories all address the question of how to change institutions—how to get courts, legal aid organizations, funders, and policymakers to behave differently.

Corpus source-specific finding

She proposes several delivery models that combine automated assistance with differentiated human support.

Exact returned source excerpt

In practice, this might involve AI-assisted self-help tools embedded in court websites that guide litigants through filing, responding, and preparing for hearings; intelligent triage systems that connect people to the right level of help (full representation, limited-scope assistance, self-help tools, or non-legal services) based on the complexity and stakes of their situation; AI-augmented legal aid workflows where attorneys handle the judgment- intensive work while AI handles drafting, research, and routine communications; and community-based “navigator” models where non-lawyers use AI tools to provide substantive legal guidance that would previously have required an attorney.

Corpus source-specific finding

AI would complement the Knowledge–Pressure–People framework through feedback, accountability, and changes to daily work.

Exact returned source excerpt

How AI Interacts with the Three-Layer Framework Importantly, AI-powered technology does not replace the Knowledge–Pressure–People framework.

Exact returned source excerpt

It interacts with all three layers: As a Knowledge Strategy, AI tools can generate unprecedented data about how people experience the justice system, what questions they ask, where they get stuck, and what outcomes they achieve—creating a real-time feedback loop that traditional research methods cannot match.

Thinking Like a Lawyer in the Age of Generative AI: Cognitive Limits on AI Adoption Among Lawyers

Academia & research · Adoption at scale (2025)

Corpus framing of this source: Cognitive limits on legal AI adoption despite demonstrated performance gains

Corpus synthesis of this source: Thinking Like a Lawyer in the Age of Generative AI: Cognitive Limits on AI Adoption Among Lawyers argues that difficulty deeply understanding AI-generated work helps explain the gap between AI’s capabilities and its limited transformation of legal practice as of mid-2025. The essay synthesises experimental, benchmarking and survey evidence, advances an explicitly exploratory account of junior lawyers’ cognitive difficulties, and recommends task- and expertise-sensitive adoption while outlining uncertain consequences for training and firm business models.

Corpus source-specific finding

AI improves task performance, but benefits vary by model and assignment.

Exact returned source excerpt

Empirical studies reveal that even outdated AI tools enable junior lawyers to complete various legal tasks 10% to 35% faster.1 More recent AI tools preserve these efficiency benefits while also substantially enhancing the quality of junior lawyers’ legal work.2 Field studies echo these findings, indicating that attorneys report meaningful productivity gains after adopting AI.3 And numerous benchmarking studies demonstrate that AI- generated responses to legal tasks frequently surpass the quality of the answers that are supplied by human lawyers who are unaided by AI.4 1 Jonathan H.

Exact returned source excerpt

In the first study, conducted at the University of Minnesota Law School, students who were given access to GPT-4—a model widely regarded as a frontier system between March 2023 and early 2024, though now considered largely obsolete—completed four assigned legal tasks approximately 5% to 38% faster, on average, than students who did not use AI.14 However, access to GPT-4 had no statistically significant effect on the quality of participants’ work product.

Corpus source-specific finding

Evidence of technical capability does not establish equivalent gains in real legal workflows.

Exact returned source excerpt

Though each type of study discussed above—randomized controlled trials, benchmarking analyses, and field experiments—has distinct methodological limitations, collectively they provide strong and consistent evidence that generative AI already possesses the technological capability to dramatically reshape legal markets.

Exact returned source excerpt

19-May-25] Thinking Like a Lawyer in Age of AI 7 These randomized controlled trials are particularly valuable for assessing AI’s current potential impact on legal practice because they directly measure how providing AI tools affects human performance.16 Given that licensing regulations, ethical norms, and established professional practices currently require licensed human lawyers to affirmatively verify and scrutinize AI- generated output, assessing AI's impact on human lawyers represents the 16 Fabrizio Dell’Acqua et al., Navigating the Jagged Technological Frontier: Field Experimental Evidence of the Effects of AI on Knowledge Worker Productivity and Quality (Sept.
